1 Introduction

This document explains how to interface a Basler rc_visard stereo camera with the Matrox
Imaging Library (MIL) software.




2 Before You Start

When installing MIL, make sure the GigE Vision option is checked.



                In recent years, Matrox Capture Assistant has been replaced by Matrox Capture
                Works, which natively supports GenICam-compliant 3D cameras.
                Therefore, Basler recommends using an up-to-date version of the MIL software.
                MIL X version 23H2 has been used for writing this document.




Interfacing rc_visard Stereo Cameras with Matrox Imaging Library (MIL)                             2
AW00190301000                                                                  Basler Application Note




3 Configuring MIL

1. Open the MIL Config application via the MIL Control Center.
2. In the navigation tree on the left, click Boards.
3. Verify that the Use Camera Discovery Service option is selected.
    This allows MIL to automatically detect added or removed cameras.




                MIL automatically adjusts the packet size setting for connected cameras.
                All other network settings for 2D GigE cameras remain unchanged (static/dynamic
                IP, jumbo frames, interrupt moderation rate, receive buffers).




Interfacing rc_visard Stereo Cameras with Matrox Imaging Library (MIL)                              3
AW00190301000                                                              Basler Application Note




4 Acquiring Images

1. In the MIL Control Center, open the Matrox Capture Works application.




    The panel on the left shows all connected cameras:




Interfacing rc_visard Stereo Cameras with Matrox Imaging Library (MIL)                          4
AW00190301000                                                                   Basler Application Note




                The same camera may appear more than once if several transport layers (.cti files)
                are installed on the system.



2. Right-click the camera instance in the GigE Vision branch and select Alloc.




                As long as the camera is allocated in Capture Works, it can’t be used by any other
                application. To release the camera, right-click the camera and select Free.



    The camera panel opens with 4 tabs. The first one is Feature Browser. That tab gives access
    to all the camera’s features:




Interfacing rc_visard Stereo Cameras with Matrox Imaging Library (MIL)                               5
AW00190301000                                                                   Basler Application Note




3. The display panel is on the right. To select which feature to display:
    1. On the Feature Browser tab, expand the Image Format Control group.
    2. Click the Component Selector feature.
    3. Select the desired component (e.g., Intensity, Disparity, or others…).
    4. Set Component Enable to True.
    5. Set Component Enable to false for all the other components.




Interfacing rc_visard Stereo Cameras with Matrox Imaging Library (MIL)                               6
AW00190301000                                                                   Basler Application Note



4. To start grabbing (single-shot or continuous), look for the corresponding icon in the toolbar.




5. In case of a continuous grab, click the same button again when you want to stop grabbing.
    Components can be enabled/disabled during grabbing. You don’t need to stop grabbing to do
    this.



                Only one component must be enabled at a time. Otherwise, the display will flicker
                or exhibit other undesired behavior during grabbing.



6. If a 3D component has been enabled (e.g., Disparity), it is possible to visualize it as a distance
   map or a point in a 3D display. To choose the view mode (2D or 3D), click the 3D and Profile
   View Controls in the View menu:




    This panel will open on the right:

AW00190301000                                                                  Basler Application Note




                The View Mode area of this panel is only visible if a 3D component has been
                enabled.
                This check is only performed by Capture Works while grabbing is stopped.

                to enable the 3D component to make the View Mode area appear. You must stop
                grabbing to force Capture Works to open the View Mode panel.

Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                                                                    1
AW00194001000                                                                    Basler Application Note




1 Introduction

It is more cost-effective to build a 5GigE machine vision system with standard components instead
of using frame grabbers, smart NICs or RDMA/RoCE V2 hardware. The use of standard
components may, however, lead to performance issues that are to be addressed.
The CPU load in a machine vision system with multiple 5GigE cameras is a key factor for the
performance of the system. The CPU load depends on both the selected hardware components
and on the system and software settings.
This application note provides the following information:
   What to observe when selecting the hardware components for a multicamera 5GigE
    machine vision system and how to set the correct parameters for Windows x86 systems.
   If you already have an installed and running multicamera 5GigE machine vision system and
    you encounter stability and CPU load problems, and error messages from the cameras
    (e.g., images get lost): How to manage the CPU load and set the correct parameters for
    Windows x86 systems.
In this application note, we assume the following setup:
   The vision system including all components is installed and functioning (e.g., 5GigE cameras,
    lenses, illumination etc.). For a multicamera system, we assume that two to four 5GigE
    cameras are installed.
   Image capture is working.
   IP addresses are set.


Further information
   For detailed information about configuring a GigE interface and driver types, refer to the Basler
    GigE Vision Network Drivers and Bandwidth Management application note (AW001445).
   Further information can also be found in the Basler Product Documentation, e.g., in the
    Network Configuration (GigE Cameras) topic.




Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                    2
AW00194001000                                                                Basler Application Note




2 Computer Hardware

The following sections provide information and recommendations for the computer hardware of a
5GigE multicamera system on Windows.


2.1     CPU

The Intel Core processors of the 10th and 11th generation work fine in a multicamera 5GigE
machine visions system.


              The Intel Core processors of the 12th, 13th, and 14th Gen have the advantage that
              image acquisition tasks (e.g., image capture) can be done on the Efficiency-cores
              (E-cores) and users can operate their image processing applications on the
              available Performance-cores (P-cores). This way, the CPU load can be managed
              more efficiently.



2.2     RAM

The required RAM speed depends on the processing which takes place after the image capture.
We strongly recommend installing the RAM in a Dual Channel mode setup, which means the use
of two separate RAM modules on the correct ports. The RAM installation in dual-channel mode
doubles the bandwidth by extending the width of the RAM interface from 64 bit to 128 bit.
Recommended minimum RAM speed:
     For up to two cameras: 2667 MHz
     For 3 or 4 cameras: DDR5 RAM with 4800 MHz or higher


2.3     NIC (Network Interface Card)

The Aquantia/Marvell chipsets and drivers provide stable operation even in multicamera setups.

We recommend the Basler 10GigE Interface Card, 1 Port (with AQC107 chipset) or the Basler
5GigE Interface Card, 4 Port (with AQC111C chipset) and the protocol driver version on the host
PC for a smooth operation (driver version 3.1.10 or latest driver version).
The Aquantia/Marvell chipsets have the advantage that a packet size for jumbo frames of 16 KB
can be used. Intel chipsets (e.g., X550) have a maximum packet size for jumbo frames of 9 KB.
Therefore, with the Marvell chipsets mentioned above, the CPU load can be significantly reduced.




Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                 3
AW00194001000                                                                      Basler Application Note




3 System and Software Settings

The following sections show you how to manage and optimize your system and software settings
to obtain a stable multicamera machine vision system with a very low CPU load.


3.1     Managing the CPU Load and Ensuring System Stability

To obtain a stable multicamera machine vision system with a reduced CPU load, make sure that
you make the settings indicated below. If there are several options for setting the
values/parameters, this is indicated for each step separately.
1. Use the latest camera firmware, with the pylon Software Suite 7.5 or newer and the latest NIC
   driver from the vendor.
      If you are not sure about the latest firmware version, contact Basler support.
2. Set the Jumbo Packet Size value to 16000.
      In pylon Viewer: Transport Layer Control category > Packet Size
      In pylon API: GevSCPSPacketSize parameter
      A larger packet size reduces the network protocol overhead and therefore reduces the CPU
      load, thus increasing the possible data rate.
      [The Intel X550 chipset only supports a packet size for jumbo frames of 9000.]
3. Set the value for the inter-packet delay to 900.
      In pylon Viewer: Transport Layer Control category > Inter-Packet Delay [ns]
      In pylon API: GevSCPD parameter
4. Enable the Windows Filter Driver.
      In pylon Viewer: Stream Parameters category > Driver Type
      The status can be checked in the Stream Grabber Parameters category.
5. Set the Interrupt Moderation Rate parameter value for the network adapter to High.
      The Interrupt Moderation Rate controls how many packets are bundled, before an interrupt is
      sent to the CPU. For some interface cards and drivers, the Extreme entry can lead to better
      results. For information about the detailed steps, see Details: Configuring the Interrupt
      Moderation Rate.
      The High value is set by default during the pylon optimization.
6. Increase the number of receive buffers for the interface card driver to the maximum, e.g., 4096:
      For information about the detailed steps, see Details: Configuring the Network Adapter Receive
      Buffers.
7. Use hyper-threading for the CPU.
   In general, hyper-threading is enabled by default. With hyper-threading, the number of threads
   is higher than the number of cores. If hyper-threading is disabled, it can be enabled in the
   BIOS of the PC.
      The status can be checked in the Windows Task Manager (Task Manager > Performance tab
      > CPU).
8. Disable unused software and unnecessary background operations such as virus scanner,
   memory compression, security services (e.g., SenseNdr.exe) etc.:




Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                      4
AW00194001000                                                                    Basler Application Note



   Check the Windows Task Manager and carefully disable unnecessary operations.
9. Increase the buffer size for pylon:
    a) Open the Windows registry editor.
    b) Go to HKEY_CURRENT_USER > SOFTWARE > Basler > pylon Viewer 64-Bit > Grab.
    c) For the MinNumBufferContinuousShot value name set the Base setting to Decimal and
       enter a minimum of 40 in the Value data field.
       Keep in mind that if the pylon buffer size is set to a high value, this may lead to a high PC
       RAM allocation and the RAM space would be allocated for grabbing tasks and couldn’t be
       used for image processing tasks. In a worst-case scenario, a running out of memory could
       happen.
    d) Click OK and quit the registry editor.
    e) Make sure that the buffer size value is correctly set in the pylon Viewer:
       Stream Parameters category > Maximum Number of Buffers entry: > 40
    f)   Close the pylon Viewer.
         Now, the increase of the buffer size for pylon is active.
10. Configure RSS (receive-side scaling) and CPU affinity.
    For information about the detailed steps, see Details: Configuring RSS (Receive-Side Scaling).



                  From Intel i12 Gen, the CPU has efficiency and performance cores that can be
                  used for different tasks.



        With RSS, one or two (not more!)* of the image grab tasks are placed on one core (e-
         core).
         *When the cameras are running at full speed, two is the maximum.
        Via adapted CPU affinity settings, it is guaranteed, that the image processing application
         runs on the other cores (p-cores).




Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                    5
AW00194001000                                                                 Basler Application Note



3.1.1 Details: Configuring the Interrupt Moderation Rate

To configure the Interrupt Moderation Rate:

1. In the pylon IP Configurator (*see note below figure):
   Select the name of the Ethernet device (network adapter) you want to configure.
2. Click Configure network adapter xxx.
3. In the … Network Adapter # … Properties dialog, go to the Advanced tab.
4. In the Property list, select Interrupt Moderation and set the Value drop-down list entry to
   Enabled (see figure below).
   Unfortunately, the indicated values in this dialog are sometimes not used by the ethernet card!
   In this case, do the following:
      If it is disabled, set it to Enable and click OK.
      If it is enabled, set it to Disabled and click OK.
       Reopen the dialog and set it to Enable and press OK.
5. In the Property list, select Interrupt Moderation Rate and set the Value drop-down list entry
   to High.
6. Repeat steps 1 to 5 for the other network adapter ports.




Note
Another possibility to configure the Interrupt Moderation Rate would be via the Windows Device
Manager (Device Manager > Network Adapters > Select name of the adapter > Change
settings).



Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                   6
AW00194001000                                                                    Basler Application Note



3.1.2 Details: Configuring the Network Adapter Receive Buffers

To configure the receive buffer for the network adapters:

1. In the pylon IP Configurator:
   Select the name of the Ethernet device (network adapter) you want to configure.
2. Click Configure network adapter xxx.
3. In the … Network Adapter # … Properties dialog, go to the Advanced tab.
4. In the Property list, select Receive buffer and enter the buffer size in the Value field to the
   maximum possible value.
5. Click OK.
6. Repeat steps 1 to 5 for the other network adapter ports.


3.1.3 Details: Configuring RSS (Receive-Side Scaling)
The RSS settings are made in the Windows PowerShell.


To configure RSS (Receive-Side Scaling):

The following description is made for four cameras, on network adapters #3, #4, #5, and #6.
Place the four network adapters on the last four threads. Up to Intel core 11th Gen, these four
threads run on two physical cores. Every physical core has two threads/logical cores.
For Intel CPUs with E-cores, the last four threads belong to E-cores.
1. Check the number of your logical CPU cores in Task Manager on the Performance tab (CPU).
   For e.g., 16 logical cores, the last four threads are #12, #13, #14, #15, as the thread numbering
   starts at #0.



                 You can display the different logical CPU cores by right-clicking in the Task
                 Manager in a CPU image > Change graph into > Logical processors.



2. Run Windows PowerShell as administrator:
   Right-click on the PowerShell program name and select Execute as administrator.
3. In Windows PowerShell, enter C:\WINDOWS\system32 and press the Enter key.
4. Set network adapter #3 to thread #12 with the following commands:
   set-netadapterrss -name “Ethernet 3” -baseprocessornumber 12
   set-netadapterrss -name “Ethernet 3” -maxprocessornumber 12
5. Use the two commands above for the other network adapters by using the following numbers:




Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                    7
AW00194001000                                                                  Basler Application Note



       For controller #4 on thread #13
       For controller #5 on thread #14
       For controller #6 on thread #15
You can check the configuration by using the get-netadapterrss command.




3.1.4 Details: Configuring the CPU Affinity

To configure the CPU affinity in the Task Manager:

1. Open the Windows Task Manager.
2. Go to the Details tab.
3. Select pylonviewer.exe and right-click on it.
4. In the pull-down menu select the Set affinity command.
    The Processor affinity dialog is opened.
5. In the Processor affinity dialog, select the CPUs you want to assign to the image processing
   task(s).
6. Click OK.




4 Verifying Your Settings/Changes

This section shows possible outcomes that can result from the changes described in the previous
sections.
For verifying your settings, observe the following points:
   The pylon Viewer can display images, but this will result in a high CPU load.
    Use the Bandwidth Manager in pylon Viewer instead for testing a real-life application scenario.
    The Bandwidth Manager (> Tools menu) grabs the images without displaying them.
   The grabbing process is finished, as soon as the complete frames are available in the “user”
    section of the RAM. After the grabbing process, the frames can be processed by the user
    application (vTools or similar tools).



Configuring a Stable Multicamera System on Windows                                                  8
AW00194001000                                                                      Basler Application Note

   Grabbing with four 5 GigE cameras in free run mode with 500 MB/s
   i7-10700 or i7-12700 CPU (Intel 10th or 12th Gen)

4.1.2 Results for an Image Processing Test Use Case
To simulate additional load on the system due to image processing, we used the Basler Barcode
Reader vTool.
With 4 x 5GigE cameras grabbing at more than 500 MB/s and a CPU load of about 70 % due to
barcode reading, we achieved stable operation.
The grab process runs on the E-cores (last four cores, configured via RSS), the barcode reading
runs on the P-cores (configured via “CPU affinity”).
Even with this high additional CPU load, the grabbing and the whole system run stably, see figure
below.

                                                                                                                                              I
Contents




II
                                             DRAFT                                              Application Notes



   1     Introduction
         Basler Camera Link, IEEE 1394 and Gigabit Ethernet (GigE) cameras are frequently installed in
         industrial applications. These applications often include devices that generate electromagnetic
         interference (EMI) and environments prone to electro-static discharge (ESD). The purpose of this
         document is to provide guidelines for avoiding problems with EMI and ESD.
         Section 2 of the document provides some guidelines that are applicable in all types of installations.
         Section 3 provides guidelines that apply to certain specific types of installations.



   2     General Guidelines for All Types of Installation
         • If possible, choose an installation that will not create ground loops: Connect to earth ground on
           only one place.
         • For optimum EMI protection, use functional earth ground (FE) in addition to protective earth
           ground (PE).



                           WARNING

                        Electric Shock
                        Electric shock may cause death or serious injury.
                        1. Functional earth ground does not protect against electric shock and
                           therefore must not be used as a substitute for protective earth ground.
                           You must provide for protective earth ground even though protective earth
                           ground is not shown in the figures of this document.


         • Consult technical literature for further information on functional earth ground. In particular, con-
           sider the following issues:
              • Materials and geometric properties (e.g. cross section, length, aspect ratio) of the con-
                ductors
              • Connections between conductors and components to be grounded
              • Design of the earth grounding system
              • Connecting functional earth ground to protective earth ground
              • Relevant regulations.
         • Use a line filter on the main power supply.
         • If possible, use a single multiple socket outlet for power supply.
         • Install the camera and camera cables as far as possible from devices that generate magnetic
           fields or sparks. If necessary, use additional shielding.
         • Always use high-quality, shielded cables.
         • Avoid placing camera cables and I/O cables parallel to wires carrying high-current, switching
           voltages such as wires supplying stepper motors or electrical devices using switching technol-
           ogy.
         • Run data- power- and I/O cables parallel to each other wherever possible. The cables should
           be run as close to each other as possible.
         • Avoid coiling camera cables and I/O cables. Try to use cables that are as short as possible. If
           the cables are too long, use a meandering path rather than coiling the cables.


Avoiding EMI and ESD Problems                                                                                  1
Application Notes                           DRAFT
          • Frequently, the electric contact between cable shield and other components, e.g. the PC chas-
            sis or the housing of a network switch must be improved. You can do this by using additional
            devices, e.g. conductive gaskets.




             Figure 1:Conductive Gasket Materials and Gaskets



          • Use ground straps for installations involving ground loops (for details, see the following sec-
            tions).




             Figure 2: Ground Strap




2                                                                           Avoiding EMI and ESD Problems
                                            DRAFT                                           Application Notes




                            Ground loops will cause unwanted electric current in your installation.
                            If possible, avoid installations involving ground loops.
                            If you can not avoid ground loops:
                            • Use ground straps and follow the recommendations given in the sections
                              below.
                            • Minimize the spatial extent of each circuit affected by a ground loop.

         • Decrease the risk of electro-static discharge by taking the following measures:
              • Use conductive materials for the place of installation (e.g. floor, workplace).
              • Use suitable clothing (cotton) and shoes.
              • Control the humidity in your environment. Low humidity can cause ESD problems.




Avoiding EMI and ESD Problems                                                                              3
Application Notes                           DRAFT
    3     Guidelines for Specific Types of Installation
          The installations listed below, consider the use of only one camera. The guidelines, however,
          apply in analogous ways also to multiple cameras.



    3.1 Installations with Camera Link Cameras
          3.1.1 Camera Attached Directly to PC, Camera Body Isolated
          From Ground
          • Make sure that the camera and the power supply are mounted on an electrical insulators.
          • Use a single multiple socket outlet for PC and power supply.
          • Make sure that the PC chassis, the equipment, and the metal mounting plates for the electrical
            insulators and attached devices (camera, power supply, see Figure 3) are connected to func-
            tional earth ground.
          • Route the Camera Link cable, the I/O cable, and the power cables as near to each other as
            possible.
          • At the point where the camera cable is plugged into the PC's frame grabber, the cable shield
            must have a very good connection to the PC chassis. The connector on a typical interface
            card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis. Usually,
            you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an electrically
            conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that makes
            good contact with the cable shield.
          • The Camera Link connector on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
          • The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
            ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
            ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
            (see Figure 3).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

          3.1.2 Camera Attached Directly to PC, Camera Body Connected
          to Ground

                            This type of installation creates earth ground loops and is very susceptible to
                            EMI and ESD. We strongly recommend avoiding this type of installation in
                            favour of an installation that will not create earth ground loops.
                            If you can not avoid ground loops, follow the instructions given below .



          • Install highly conductive ground straps between the PC chassis, line filter chassis and the
            metal racks where the camera and the power supply are mounted (see Figures 2 and 4).
          • Route the camera cable, the I/O cable, and the power cable as near to the ground straps as
            possible.
          • Use a single multiple socket outlet for PC and power supply.
          • Make sure that the equipment and the chassis of PC and line filter are connected to functional
            earth ground.
          • At the point where the camera cable is plugged into the PC's frame grabber, the cable shield
            must have a very good connection to the PC chassis. The connector on a typical interface
            card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis. Usually,
            you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an electrically
            conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that makes
            good contact with the cable shield.
          • The Camera Link connector on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
          • The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
            ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
            ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
            (see Figure 4).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

    3.2 Installations with IEEE 1394 Cameras
          3.2.1 Camera Attached Directly to PC, Camera Body Isolated
          From Ground
          • Make sure that the camera is mounted on an electrical insulator.
          • Make sure that the PC chassis, the equipment, and the metal mounting plate for the electrical
            insulator and the camera (see Figure 5) are connected to functional earth ground.
          • At the point where the camera cable is plugged into the PC's 1394 interface card, the cable
            shield must have a very good connection to the PC chassis. The connector on a typical inter-
            face card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis.
            Usually, you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an elec-
            trically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that
            makes good contact with the cable shield.
          • The IEEE 1394 connector on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
          • The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
            ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
            ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
            (see Figure 5).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

Avoiding EMI and ESD Problems                                                                  11
Application Notes                            DRAFT
          3.2.3 Camera Attached to PC via Hub, Camera Body and Hub
          Isolated From Ground
          • Make sure that the camera, the hub and its power supply are mounted on electrical insulators.
          • Use a single multiple socket outlet for the PC and the power supply for the hub.
          • Make sure that the PC chassis, the equipment, and the metal mounting plates for the electrical
            insulators and attached devices (camera, hub, power supply, see Figure 7) are connected to
            functional earth ground.
          • Always use a hub and a power supply for the hob with conductive bodies. This will allow EMI
            or ESD to flow over the bodies of the devices rather than through their circuitry. If you can’t get
            the devices with conductive bodies, see Section 3.4.
          • Make sure that the hub power supply is isolated, i.e., its AC input is not connected to earth
            ground. If the power supply has a metal body, mount the power supply on an electrical insula-
            tor.
          • Make sure that the power supply for the hub and the power cables for the power supply are
            not routed near wires carrying high-current, switching voltages such as wires supplying step-
            per motors or electrical devices using switching technology. Place the power supply unit for
            the hub near to the camera cabling. Route the power cables for the hub power supply near to
            the camera cabling.
          • At each point where a camera cable connects to the hub, the cable shield must have a very
            good connection to the hub housing. Typically, you must make this connection better by using
            an additional device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
            attached to the hub housing and that makes good contact with the cable shield.
          • At the point where the camera cable is plugged into the PC's 1394 interface card, the cable
            shield must have a very good connection to the PC chassis. The connector on a typical inter-
            face card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis.
            Usually, you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an elec-
            trically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that
            makes good contact with the cable shield.
          • The IEEE 1394 connector on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
          • The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
            ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
            ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
            (see Figure 7).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

          3.2.4 Camera Attached to PC via Hub, Camera Body and Hub
          Connected to Ground

                            This type of installation creates earth ground loops and is very susceptible to
                            EMI and ESD. We strongly recommend avoiding this type of installation in
                            favour of an installation that will not create earth ground loops.
                            If you can not avoid ground loops, follow the instructions given below .



          • Install highly conductive ground straps connecting the PC chassis and the metal racks where
            the camera, the hub, and the power supply for the hub are mounted (see Figures 2 and 8).
          • Route the camera cables, the I/O cable, and the power cables as near to the ground straps as
            possible.
          • Place the hub power supply near to the hub.
          • Use a single multiple socket outlet for PC and power supply for the hub.
          • Make sure that the PC chassis, the equipment, and the metal racks are connected to func-
            tional earth ground.
          • Always use hubs and power supplies with conductive bodies. This will allow EMI or ESD to
            flow over the hub body rather than through their circuitry. If you can’t get a hub or power sup-
            ply with a conductive body, see Section 3.4.
          • Make sure that the power supply for the hub and the power cable for the power supply are not
            routed near wires carrying high-current, switching voltages such as wires supplying stepper
            motors or electrical devices using switching technology.
          • At each point where a camera cable connects to the hub, the cable shield must have a very
            good connection to the hub housing. Typically, you must make this connection better by using
            an additional device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
            attached to the hub housing and that makes good contact with the cable shield.
          • At the point where the camera cable is plugged into the PC’s 1394 interface card, the cable
            shield must have a very good connection to the PC chassis. The connector on a typical inter-
            face card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis.
            Usually, you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an elec-
            trically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that
            makes good contact with the cable shield.
          • The IEEE 1394 connector on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
          • The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
            ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
            ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
            (see Figure 8).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

     3.3 Installations with GigE Cameras
          3.3.1 Camera Attached Directly to PC, Camera Body Isolated
          From Ground
          • Make sure that the camera, the power supply, and the line filter are mounted on electrical insu-
            lators.
          • Use a single multiple socket outlet for PC and power supply.
          • Make sure that the PC chassis, the equipment and the metal mounting plates for the electrical
            insulators and attached devices (camera, power supply, line filter, see Figure 9) are connected
            to functional earth ground.
          • Route the GigE cable, the I/O cable, and the power cables as near to each other as possible.
          • At the point where the camera cable is plugged into the PC's GigE interface card, the cable
            shield must have a very good connection to the PC chassis. The connector on a typical inter-
            face card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis.
            Usually, you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an elec-
            trically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that
            makes good contact with the cable shield.
          • The GigE connector (RJ-45) on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
          • The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
            ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
            ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
            (see Figure 9).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

Application Notes                            DRAFT
          3.3.3 Camera Attached to PC via Network Switch, Camera Body
          and Network Switch Isolated From Ground
          • Make sure that the camera, the power supply, the network switch, and the line filter are
            mounted on electrical insulators.
          • Use a single multiple socket outlet for PC, network switch, and power supply.
          • Make sure that the PC chassis, the equipment, and the metal mounting plates for the electrical
            insulators and attached devices (camera, power supply, network switch, line filter, see Figure
            11) are connected to functional earth ground.
          • Route the GigE cable, the I/O cable, and the power cables as near to each other as possible.
          • Always use a power supply, a network switch, and a line filter with conductive bodies. This will
            allow EMI or ESD to flow over the bodies of the devices rather than through their circuitry. If
            you can’t get a power supply, a network switch, or a line filter with conductive bodies, see Sec-
            tion 3.4.
          • Make sure that the network switch power supply is isolated, i.e., its AC input is not connected
            to earth ground. If the power supply has a metal body, mount the power supply on an electrical
            insulator.
          • Make sure that the power supplies for the network switch and for the camera and the power
            cables for the power supply are not routed near wires carrying high-current, switching voltages
            such as wires supplying stepper motors or electrical devices using switching technology. Place
            the power supply units for the network switch and for the camera near to the camera cabling.
            Route the power cables for the power supply and for the network switch near to the camera
            cabling.
          • At each point where a camera cable connects to the network switch, the cable shield must
            have a very good connection to the network switch housing. Typically, you must make this
            connection better by using an additional device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see
            Figure 1) that is securely attached to the network switch housing and that makes good contact
            with the cable shield.
          • At each point where a power cable connects to the power supply, the cable shield must have a
            very good connection to the power supply housing. Typically, you must make this connection
            better by using an additional device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1)
            that is securely attached to the power supply housing and that makes good contact with the
            cable shield.
          • At the point where the camera cable is plugged into the PC's interface card, the cable shield
            must have a very good connection to the PC chassis. The connector on a typical interface
            card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis. Usually,
            you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an electrically
            conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that makes
            good contact with the cable shield.
          • The GigE connector (RJ-45) on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
          • The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
            ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
            ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
            (see Figure 11).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

            card usually does not provide enough contact between the shield and the PC chassis. Usually,
            you must make this connection better by using an additional device such as an electrically
            conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely attached to the PC chassis and that makes
            good contact with the cable shield.
          • The GigE connector (RJ-45) on the camera is specially designed to provide good contact
            between the camera cable shield and the camera body. No modifications to this connection
            are necessary.
             The I/O ground in the camera is isolated from the camera ground. Therefore, the cable shield-
             ing on the I/O cable should only be connected at the camera OR at your equipment. We rec-
             ommend connecting the cable shielding at the camera and insulating it from the equipment
             (see Figure 12).
             If you choose to connect the I/O cable shield at your equipment, the cable shield must have a
             very good connection to the equipment. Typically, you must make this connection better by
             using a device such as an electrically conductive gasket (see Figure 1) that is securely
             attached to your equipment and that makes good contact with the cable shield.

Application Notes                            DRAFT
     3.4 Using a Hub, Network Switch, Power Supply or Line Fil-
         ter with a Non-conductive Body
          If you use a hub, a network switch, a power supply, or a line filter, we strongly recommend using
          these devices with conductive bodies. This will allow EMI or ESD to flow over the bodies of the
          devices rather than through their circuitry. In some areas, however, it may be very difficult to
          purchase the devices with conductive bodies. If you find that you must use a device with a non-
          conductive body, you can modify the device so that it will have reasonable EMI and ESD
          resistance.
          When making the modification, your goal is to provide a highly conductive path between the cable
          shields on the incoming connectors and the cable shield on the outgoing connector on the device.
          Typically, you can do this by using something such as a piece of electrically conductive gasket that
          runs between the incoming and the outgoing connectors (see Figure 13). The conductor must
          make good contact with each of the connectors.
          Note: You may find that the device has an internal connection that runs between the incoming
          connectors and the outgoing connector. This internal connection is not sufficient to avoid EMI or
          ESD problems with the device.

                            must have good contact
                            with connectors

APPLICATION NOTE

    Interfacing Basler GigE Cameras
    With Cognex VisionPro 7.2

    This Application Note explains how to interface a Basler camera with GigE
    interface with the Cognex VisionPro 7.2 software package using a standard
    Gigabit Ethernet card.

    A.) Hardware Requirements:

    Beside the camera and its power supply itself, you still need an Ethernet
    cable (Cat 6 or higher) and your PC must be equipped with a Gigabit
    Ethernet card (Network Interface Connector = NIC). We recommend using
    a NIC with Intel PRO/1000 chipset.

    The drivers for your NIC must have been installed properly before starting
    with the installation of your Cognex VisionPro software package.

    B.) Software Installation Requirements:

    After the installation of the Cognex VisionPro software package the instal-
    lation process for the Cognex Drivers must be launched:




                                       1
APPLICATION NOTE




           If you decide to select “Custom” as the setup type, as shown in the follow-
           ing dialog, make sure that the “Cognex GigE Interface for Cameras” driver
           is selected to be installed:




                                               2
APPLICATION NOTE




           C.) Configuring the Local Area Connection (LAN):
           By default, Basler GigE cameras are not configured for a static IP address.
           We recommend configuring the NIC for obtaining an IP address automati-
           cally too. In the “Properties” dialog box of the LAN connection you want to
           configure, select “Internet Protocol (TCP/IP)” and then press the
           “Properties” button:




                                              3
APPLICATION NOTE




           In the “Internet Protocol (TCP/IP) Properties” dialog box, select “Obtain an
           IP address automatically” and press the “OK” button:




           Of course, if your camera should be configured for a static IP address and if
           you know this address, you may also configure the LAN connection for a
           static IP address in the same subnet which is used by the camera.




                                               4
APPLICATION NOTE




           D.) The Cognex GigE Vision Configuration Tool

           Once you have connected your camera to your PC, please run the “Cognex
           GigE Vision Configuration Tool”. You will find a list of all of your LAN
           connections displayed. Select the network connection (LAN) you like to
           configure. You’ll see the following dialog:




           You can change the adapter’s IP address and Subnet mask here, if needed.

           Also, you will see the status of your firewall, which should be “Off” in order
           to be able to receive image data from your camera. As an alternative, you
           can configure your firewall to unblock certain ports, see “E.) Firewall
           Configuration” for details.




                                                5
APPLICATION NOTE




           If your NIC supports Jumbo Frames you should configure your adapter to
           use the maximum Jumbo Frame size (MTU) for best performance. For an
           Intel PRO/1000 adapter you will find the “Jumbo Frames” setting in the
           “Advanced” tab in the adapter’s “Properties” dialog:




                                            6
APPLICATION NOTE




           Selecting your camera device enables you to change the IP address and
           Subnet mask of your camera. Please keep in mind that the IP address of
           your camera must be in the same address range as the IP address of the
           LAN connection to which the camera is connected to. The Subnet mask of
           your camera device needs to be the same as the Subnet mask of your LAN
           connection. The following dialog shows the configuration of a connected
           camera:




                                            7
APPLICATION NOTE




           E.) Firewall Configuration

           Any application using the GigE Vision network protocol must be able to
           accept data from the camera on UDP port 3956. On systems with the
           Windows firewall enabled, this can be achieved by selecting “Unblock”
           when the operating system notifies the user that the VisionPro application
           needs to accept connections from the network. To ensure optimal perfor-
           mance, UDP ports 49153 through 49169 should also be unblocked. If you
           are using the Windows firewall, you can unblock these ports by clicking on
           the “Windows Firewall” icon in the control panel, switching to the
           “Exceptions” tab, and clicking the “Add Port” button to unblock UDP ports
           between 49153 and 49169.

           F.) Configuring the Camera / Grabbing Images with VisionPro
           Quickbuild

           An easy test for successful interfacing between VisionPro and a Basler
           GigE camera can be performed using the VisionPro Quickbuild application.

           After starting the VisionPro Quickbuild application, double click on the
           Image Source item:




                                               8
APPLICATION NOTE




           The Image Source dialog box will show up. As source, select Camera and
           then select your camera device from the Image Acquisition Device drop-
           down box. If you click on the arrow of the Video Formats dropdown box, a
           context menu will show up, listing the available video formats, which might
           be Bayer Color and/or Mono for Generic GigE Vision devices. Finally, press
           the Initialize Acquisition button. Once the acquisition is initialized, you can
           close the Image Source dialog box (it can be opened again by double
           clicking Image Source):




                                                9
APPLICATION NOTE




                        Back in the QuickBuild dialog box press the “Run Job Continuously” button.
                        You should now see a live image, as shown in the screenshot below:

                        samples folder:
